Orbit Elements from Kepler Solutions in Projective Coordinates
Joseph T.A. Peterson, Manoranjan Majji, John L. Junkins

Abstract
Closed-Form Kepler solutions in projective coordinates are used to define a corresponding set of eight orbit elements and obtain their governing equations for arbitrarily-perturbed two-body dynamics. The elements and their dynamics are singularity-free in all cases besides rectilinear motion (when angular momentum vanishes). The classic J 2 J_{2} -perturbed two-body problem is developed and used for numerical verification.



INTRODUCTION

Projective coordinate transformations,
combined with an appropriate transformation of the evolution parameter
1
1

1

In particular, a parameter
s
defined by
{\mathrm{d}}t=r^{2}{\mathrm{d}}s
, or a parameter
\tau
defined by
{\mathrm{d}}t=(r^{2}/{\ell}){\mathrm{d}}\tau
where
{\ell}
is the (specific) angular momentum magnitude, making
\tau
the true anomaly up to an additive constant.
,
are known to transform the nonlinear second-order dynamics of the
3
-dim Kepler problem to those of a
4
-dim linear oscillator
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
]
.
Previous work by Ferrándiz et al.
[
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
]
and Deprit et al.
[
10
]
extended such dimension-raising point transformations of configuration coordinates to canonical transformations of phase space coordinates in the Hamiltonian framework. We refer to Ferrándiz’s transformation as the Burdet-Ferrándiz (BF) transformation (following the convention of
[
10
]
) as it is a “canonical extension” of Burdet’s initial projective point transformation
[
1
,
2
,
4
]
.

The present authors developed their own canonically-extended projective transformation in
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
]
(with a geometric formulation given in
[
15
]
), which they feel is comparatively more simple and intuitive in both its final form and its derivation. In particular, it possesses an immediate connection to orbital reference frames and attitude kinematics, with the angular momentum matrix playing a prominent role.

This paper is an extension of the aforementioned work in projective-based linear regularization of orbital dynamics. Given that the Kepler problem has readily-available closed-form solutions in projective coordinates, it is only natural to use these solutions to define a corresponding set of orbit elements and obtain their governing equations of motion for the arbitrarily-perturbed Kepler problem. That is the focus of this work. It should be noted, however, that the orbit elements introduced here are not action-angle coordinates obtained from the Hamilton-Jacobi equation, and that our treatment of the elements (section
2
) deviates from the Hamiltonian framework in which the projective coordinate regularization was previously formulated in
[
11
,
12
,
15
]
(as reviewed in section
1
of this work.).
We further note that much of this work will use a set of “quasi-canonical” projective coordinates where one of the conjugate momenta coordinates is exchanged for a quasi-momenta coordinate.

Outline.

This paper is outlined as follows:

1.

In section
1
, we review the projective coordinate canonical transformation developed in
[
11
,
12
,
13
]
(see
[
15
]
or latter chapters of
[
12
]
for a more mathematical, geometric, formulation). The Hamiltonian dynamics for perturbed central-force motion are given, along with closed-form solutions for pure Kepler dynamics. We note that this linearizing transformation also involves a transformation of the evolution parameter. Two such parameter are given, both achieving linearization: (1) a parameter
s
defined by
{\mathrm{d}}t=r^{2}{\mathrm{d}}s
, and (2) a parameter
\tau
defined by
{\mathrm{d}}t=(r^{2}/{\ell}){\mathrm{d}}\tau
(making
\tau
the true anomaly up to an additive constant).

2.

In section
2
, we use the closed-form
\tau
-parameterized Kepler solutions in projective coordinates — with one conjugate momentum coordinate exchanged for a quasi-momenta coordinate — to define a set of orbit elements which, in the unperturbed case, correspond to initial conditions. Variation of parameter is used to obtain the governing equations of motion, which are singularity-free in all cases besides rectilinear motion (when
{\ell}=0
). The
J_{2}
-perturbed two-body problem is presented and used for numerical verification.

•

Appendix
A
contains some useful relations involving the angular momentum, and its partial derivatives, in projective coordinates.
Appendix
B
contains state transition matrices and other matrix relations for Kepler dynamics in projective coordinates (these are used in section
2
). Appendix
C
contains a different approach to the orbit elements that was given previously by the authors. Appendix
D
outlines some properties of Hamiltonian systems with a transformation of the evolution parameter.

Notation & Conventions.

This work is written in the coordinate-dominant manner of classic analytical dynamics with all transformations framed as “passive” coordinate transformations. Our mathematical arena is only ever regarded as some
n
-dim real coordinate vector space,
{\mathbb{R}}^{{\textit{{{n}}}}}
, equipped with the usual structures, operations, and abuses of notation.

In addition, we note the following general notations and conventions:

•

Matrix notation is used frequently, along with occasional “cartesian index notation”. For the latter, all indices appear as subscripts and summation is implied over any repeated indices.
Almost always, Latin indices such as
i,j,k,l
range from
1
to
3
.

•

Expressions such as
{{\boldsymbol{u}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{{\textit{{{n}}}}}
are often an abuse of notation indicating that
{{\boldsymbol{u}}}
is an object that
takes values in

{\mathbb{R}}^{{\textit{{{n}}}}}
. For any such
{{\boldsymbol{u}}}
, we make no distinction between
{{\boldsymbol{u}}}
as an ordered
n
-tuple,
(u_{1},\dots,u_{{\textit{{{n}}}}})
, and
{{\boldsymbol{u}}}
as a column vector,
[u_{1}\dots u_{{\textit{{{n}}}}}]^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsf{{T}}}}}
.

•

The norm of some
{{\boldsymbol{u}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{{\textit{{{n}}}}}
is denoted by

u:=|\!|{{\boldsymbol{u}}}|\!|:=\sqrt{{{\boldsymbol{u}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}}
, and the normalized unit vector by
{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{u}}}}}:=\tfrac{1}{u}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}
such that
{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{u}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{u}}}}}=1
.

•

The “dot product” denotes contraction in the usual sense on
{\mathbb{R}}^{{\textit{{{n}}}}}
. That is, for
{{\boldsymbol{u}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{{\textit{{{n}}}}}
, then
{{\boldsymbol{u}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}=1_{ab}u_{a}{\nu}_{b}=u_{a}{\nu}_{a}
(for
a,b=1,\dots,{{\textit{{{n}}}}}
). Contraction with some
M\in{\mathbb{R}}^{{{\textit{{{n}}}}}\times{{\textit{{{n}}}}}}
is denoted either with or without a dot. That is,
M{{\boldsymbol{u}}}=M{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}=M_{ab}u_{b}\hat{\boldsymbol{{1}}}_{a}
and
M^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsf{{T}}}}}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}=M^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsf{{T}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}={{\boldsymbol{u}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}M=M_{ab}u_{a}\hat{\boldsymbol{{1}}}_{b}=M_{ba}u_{b}\hat{\boldsymbol{{1}}}_{a}
.

•

Tensor products,
\otimes
, and exterior/wedge products,
\wedge
, also have their usual interpretation on
{\mathbb{R}}^{{\textit{{{n}}}}}
. In particular, the tensor product of any
{{\boldsymbol{u}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{{\textit{{{n}}}}}
is equivalent to the matrix
{{\boldsymbol{u}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}={{\boldsymbol{u}}}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsf{{T}}}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{{{\textit{{{n}}}}}\times{{\textit{{{n}}}}}}
, and the exterior product is equivalent to the antisymmetric matrix
{{\boldsymbol{u}}}\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\wedge\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}={{\boldsymbol{u}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}-{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}={{\boldsymbol{u}}}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsf{{T}}}}}-{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsf{{T}}}}}\in\mathfrak{so}^{{{\textit{{{n}}}}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbb{R}}}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{{{\textit{{{n}}}}}\times{{\textit{{{n}}}}}}
.

•

Hodge dual on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.
For any
{{\boldsymbol{u}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, the Hodge dual,
{{\boldsymbol{u}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}\in\mathfrak{so}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbb{R}}}}
, is the antisymmetric matrix with components given in terms of the 3-dim Levi-Civita permutation symbol by
u^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}_{ij}:={{\textit{{{e}}}}}_{ijk}u_{k}
. For
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, this differs from the usual “axial dual” or “cross product matrix”,
{{{\boldsymbol{u}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\times}}}
, only by a sign:
{{\boldsymbol{u}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}=-{{{\boldsymbol{u}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\times}}}
. We note the following relations, specific to
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
:

\displaystyle\;\;\begin{array}[]{lcll}u^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}_{ij}:={{\textit{{{e}}}}}_{ijk}u_{k}&\leftrightarrow&u_{i}=\tfrac{1}{2}{{\textit{{{e}}}}}_{ijk}u^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}_{jk}\\[3.0pt]
({{\boldsymbol{u}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}={{\boldsymbol{u}}}\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\wedge\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}&\leftrightarrow&({{\boldsymbol{u}}}\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\wedge\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}={{\boldsymbol{u}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}\end{array}

,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lll}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}=-{{\boldsymbol{u}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}={{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}=-{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}={{\boldsymbol{u}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}\\[3.0pt]
{{\boldsymbol{u}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{w}}}={{\boldsymbol{u}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}({{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{w}}})\end{array}

,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lll}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star\star}}={{\boldsymbol{u}}}\\[3.0pt]
{{\boldsymbol{u}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}={{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{{\boldsymbol{u}}}-({{\boldsymbol{u}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}){\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}\end{array}\;\;

•

A note on “
y=y(x)
”.
We (ab)use the notation
y=y(x)
, or just
y(x)
, to indicate there is something called
y
that is a “function of” something called
x
. Similarly, under some transformation
x\leftrightarrow z
, the notation
y(x)
and
y(z)
should be interpreted, respectively, as “
y
expressed in terms of
x
” and “
y
expressed in terms of
z
”.

•

A note on mass.
Throughout this work, the mass is scaled out of the Hamiltonian, kinetic/potential energy, forces, angular momentum, etc. All such quantities are given per unit mass (or, for two-body dynamics, per unit

reduced
mass,
m:=\tfrac{m_{a}m_{b}}{m_{a}+m_{b}}
). Often, we do not state this explicitly. For instance, when we refer to the angular momentum we always mean the

specific
2
2

2

The term “specific angular momentum” means the angular momentum per unit mass (or per unit reduced mass). The same applies to terms such as “specific energy”.

angular momentum.

•

A note on inertial cartesian velocity vs. momentum coordinates.
These are equivalent for the purposes of this work wherein the (reduced) mass is scaled out of everything. As such, there is no difference, in practice, between
inertial cartesian
velocity and momentum coordinates.
3
3

3

Though, in mathematical/geometric terms, there
is
still a difference: velocity coordinates are functions on velocity phase space (a tangent bundle) whereas momentum coordinates are functions on phase space (a cotangent bundle) and they therefore cannot be mathematically equal, even in the inertial cartesian case. We ignore such details in this work.

1.  ORBITAL DYNAMICS IN PROJECTIVE COORDINATES

We present an overview of the canonically-extended projective coordinate transformation and its application to regularization and linearization of central-force particle dynamics — in particular, Kepler-type dynamics.
The following does not include anything not already addressed by the authors in
[
11
,
12
,
15
]
, where the derivations and details can be found.
The impatient reader may skip to section
2
— where we detail the orbit elements — and refer back to section
1
as needed.

Original Cartesian Coordinate Formulation.

Let
({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
be cartesian position and velocity
(or momentum
4
4

4

In this work, there is no difference, in practice, between inertial cartesian velocity and momentum coordinates since the mass is scaled out. We stress that this is true only for
inertial cartesian
velocity and momentum coordinates.
)
coordinates in an orthonormal inertial frame.
We start with the Hamiltonian and canonical equations of motion for a particle in Euclidean 3-space, subject to conservative forces corresponding to some central-force potential function,
V^{0}(r)
, that depends only on the radial distance.
We also allow for any additional arbitrary perturbing forces with cartesian components
{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
.
The Hamiltonian dynamics in cartesian coordinates
({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
are then:

\displaystyle{{K}}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}{\nu}^{2}\,+\,V^{0}(r)

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}\dot{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}\,=\,\tfrac{\uppartial{{K}}}{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}}\,=\,{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}&,\hskip 18.49988pt\dot{{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}}\,=\,-\tfrac{\uppartial{{K}}}{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}+{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\,=\,-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial r}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}+{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\end{array}

where
r:=|\!|{{\boldsymbol{r}}}|\!|
and
{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}:={{\boldsymbol{r}}}/r
.
The perturbing forces may be conservative and/or nonconservative, that is:

\displaystyle{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\,=\,-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{1}}{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}+{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}}

(8)

where
V^{1}=V^{1}({{\boldsymbol{r}}},t)
is a potential function modeling any arbitrary conservative forces (e.g., higher-order gravity terms or third body terms), and
{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
are the cartesian components of any
non
conservative forces (e.g., thrust or drag).

•

Conservative perturbations could (and usually would) be included simply by adding their potential,
V^{1}
, to the Hamiltonian function.
5
5

5

With
V^{1}
included in
{{K}}
, Hamilton’s equation for
{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}
takes the form
\dot{{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}}=-\tfrac{\uppartial{{K}}}{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}+{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}}=-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial r}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{1}}{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}+{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}}
, which is equivalent to
\dot{{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}}=-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial r}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}+{{\boldsymbol{F}}}
.

Yet, since all perturbations are completely arbitrary for now, it is easier to lump them all into one object,
{{\boldsymbol{F}}}
, as in Eq.(
8
).

•

When the form of
V^{0}(r)
is relevant (for many developments, it is not), then we consider
a Kepler-type potential:
6
6

6

We note that the transformation used in this work is equally effective at linearizing potentials of the more general
Manev
-type,
V^{0}=-{\textnormal{{{k}}}}_{1}/{r}-\tfrac{1}{2}{\textnormal{{{k}}}}_{2}/{r^{2}}
, for scalars
{\textnormal{{{k}}}}_{1},{\textnormal{{{k}}}}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. This was considered by the authors in
[
11
,
15
]
. In this work, we limit consideration to Kepler-type dynamics (with perturbations).

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc}{\text{\scriptsize{Kepler-type}}}\\
{\text{\scriptsize{potential}}}\end{array}

\displaystyle V^{0}\,=\,-{\textnormal{{{k}}}}_{1}/r\hskip 18.49988pt,\hskip 18.49988pt{\textnormal{{{k}}}}_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}

1.1  A Canonical Transformation for Projective Coordinates

The transformation summarized in the following is a modified version of the BF transformation
[
6
]
that was detailed by the authors in
[
11
,
12
,
15
]
. Our transformation differs slightly from the BF transformation at the configuration level, and significantly at the momentum level.

A Canonically-Extended Projective Transformation.

We transform from the above cartesian coordinates
({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
to
new, redundant, coordinates
({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{8}
where
{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}
are configuration coordinates with conjugate momentum coordinates
{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}
. We partition these coordinates as:
7
7

7

Later, we will modify the coordinate ordering to
({{\boldsymbol{q}}},{{\boldsymbol{p}}},u,p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})
but, for now, this does not matter.

\displaystyle({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}})=({{\boldsymbol{q}}},u,{{\boldsymbol{p}}},p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{8}\hskip 18.49988pt,\hskip 18.49988pt{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}=({{\boldsymbol{q}}},u)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\hskip 18.49988pt,\hskip 18.49988pt{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}=({{\boldsymbol{p}}},p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}

(11)

The transformation begins by specifying a point transformation
{\gamma}:{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\mapsto{{\boldsymbol{r}}}
subject to a constraint:

\displaystyle{\gamma}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\;,\hskip 18.49988pt{{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,=\,{\gamma}({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}})\,=\,\tfrac{1}{u}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\;,

\displaystyle{\text{\scriptsize{subject to}}}\;\;\varphi({{\boldsymbol{q}}})=|\!|{{\boldsymbol{q}}}|\!|-1=0\;,

\displaystyle{\textrm{{\footnotesize{rnk}}}\hskip 1.42262pt}{\text{{{\small{{d}}}}}}{\gamma}=3\hskip 9.24994pt

(12)

where
{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}:={{\boldsymbol{q}}}/q
with
q:=|\!|{{\boldsymbol{q}}}|\!|\neq|\!|{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}|\!|
.
We
specify
the above point transformation and constraint, which then induce a conjugate momentum coordinate transformation as detailed by the authors in
[
11
,
12
]
. In short: associating to
\varphi
a Langrage multiplier
\lambda
(which serves like an “extra” momentum coordinate), then we obtain a momentum coordinate transformation
{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}=({{\boldsymbol{p}}},p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})\leftrightarrow({{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}},\lambda)
as follows
[
11
]
:

\displaystyle{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}=B^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsf{{T}}}}}\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}\\
\lambda\end{pmatrix}\hskip 9.24994pt\leftrightarrow\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}\\
\lambda\end{pmatrix}=B^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsf{{--T}}}}}{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}\hskip 18.49988pt,\hskip 18.49988ptB=\begin{pmatrix}\tfrac{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}{\uppartial{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}}\\[5.0pt]
\tfrac{\uppartial\varphi}{\uppartial{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{4\times 4}

(13)

The point transformation in Eq.(
12
) then has a “canonical extension”,
{\Gamma}:({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}})\mapsto({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})
, with
{\textrm{{\footnotesize{rnk}}}\hskip 1.42262pt}{\text{{{\small{{d}}}}}}{\Gamma}=6
,
given
by:
8
8

8

The point transformation
{\gamma}:{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\ni({{\boldsymbol{q}}},u)\mapsto{{\boldsymbol{r}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
is
a submersion; it is surjective with
\text{rnk}{\text{{{\small{{d}}}}}}{\gamma}\equiv\text{rnk}\tfrac{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}{\uppartial({{\boldsymbol{q}}},u)}=3
. It is then lifted to a “canonical submersion”,
{\Gamma}:{\mathbb{R}}^{8}\ni({{\boldsymbol{q}}},u,{{\boldsymbol{p}}},p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})\mapsto({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
, which is surjective with
\text{rnk}{\text{{{\small{{d}}}}}}{\Gamma}\equiv{\textrm{{\footnotesize{rnk}}}\hskip 1.42262pt}\tfrac{\uppartial({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})}{\uppartial({{\boldsymbol{q}}},u,{{\boldsymbol{p}}},p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})}=6
. Note these are actually
local
submersions as the domain and codomains of these maps are not all of the indicated
{\mathbb{R}}^{{\textit{{{n}}}}}
; they are subsets excluding the cases
u=0
and
q=|\!|{{\boldsymbol{q}}}|\!|=0
.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc}{\Gamma}:{\mathbb{R}}^{8}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{6}\\[3.0pt]
({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}})\mapsto({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})\end{array}\;\left\{\;\;\boxed{\begin{array}[]{rlllll}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}&\!\!\!=\,\tfrac{1}{u}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\\[5.0pt]
{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}&\!\!\!=\,uq({\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\,-\,u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\;=\,-u({{\boldsymbol{q}}}\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\wedge\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}-u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\end{array}}\right.

,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllll}\varphi=q-1=0\\[6.0pt]
\lambda={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\end{array}\hskip 18.49988pt

where the constraint
\varphi
is built into the derivation of the momenta transformation, which includes the above Lagrange multiplier
\lambda

associated with
\varphi
.
9
9

9

In previous work, the authors instead posed the constraint function as
\varphi=\tfrac{1}{2}(q^{2}-1)=0
. This changes little other than the fact that the associated
\lambda
is then instead given by
\lambda=\tfrac{1}{q^{2}}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
(as opposed to the present, equivalent, formulation with
\lambda=\tfrac{1}{q}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
).

A method of inverting the above is given below in Eq.(
1.1
).
First, we note that since the transformation in Eq.(
1.1
) is not time-dependent, then it takes the cartesian coordinate Hamiltonian,
{{K}}
, to a projective coordinate Hamiltonian,
{{H}}
, given simply by direct substitution,
{{H}}={{K}}\circ{\Gamma}
. This leads to:

\displaystyle{{K}}=\tfrac{1}{2}{\nu}^{2}+V^{0}(r)\hskip 18.49988pt\Rightarrow\hskip 18.49988pt\boxed{{{H}}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}u^{2}\big({\ell}^{2}+u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}\big)\,+\,V^{0}(u)}\hskip 18.49988pt,\hskip 18.49988pt{\ell}^{2}=q^{2}p^{2}-({{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}})^{2}

(20)

where
{\ell}^{2}
is the angular momentum magnitude (noted below), and where our (ab)use of notation
V^{0}(u)
simply indicates the original
V^{0}(r)
expressed in the new coordinates using
{{\boldsymbol{r}}}=\tfrac{1}{u}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}
and, thus,
r=|\!|{{\boldsymbol{r}}}|\!|=1/u
.

\,\scriptscriptstyle{\blacksquare}\,

Angular momentum.
It is worth noting that the
angular momentum,
{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}={{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}={{\boldsymbol{r}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}
, takes the same form when expressed in terms of
({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})
or
({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}})
. It is independent of
(u,p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})
, depending only on
({{\boldsymbol{q}}},{{\boldsymbol{p}}})
:
10
10

10

Note
{\ell}_{ij}={{\textit{{{e}}}}}_{ijk}{\ell}_{k}\leftrightarrow{\ell}_{i}=\tfrac{1}{2}{{\textit{{{e}}}}}_{ijk}{\ell}_{jk}
are Hodge duals of one another. We write simply
{\ell}_{ij}
rather than
{\ell}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}_{ij}
.

\displaystyle{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}\,=\,{{\boldsymbol{r}}}\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\wedge\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}\,=\,{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\wedge\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}

,

\displaystyle{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}\,=\,{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,=\,{{\boldsymbol{p}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}

,

\displaystyle{\ell}^{2}\,=\,r^{2}{\nu}^{2}-({{\boldsymbol{r}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})^{2}\,=\,q^{2}p^{2}-({{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}})^{2}

(21)

Some useful angular momentum relations are given Appendix
A
.

\,\scriptscriptstyle{\blacksquare}\,

Two “extra” integrals of motion.

It was shown in
[
11
,
12
,
13
,
15
]
that the new Hamiltonian
{{H}}
for the redundant phase space coordinates,
({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{8}
, permits two additional integrals
of motion.
11
11

11

“Integral of motion” is perhaps misleading terminology in this context; the functions in Eq.(

■

‣
1.1
) do not represent a conserved physical quantity for a particle moving in Euclidean 3-space (the actual system we are modeling). They are, rather, “kinematic constants” which arise from artificially increasing the dimension of phase space. However, in a metathetical sense, they are indeed true integrals of motion for the new Hamiltonian system described by
{{H}}
.

These are:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc}{\text{\scriptsize{integrals}}}\\
{\text{\scriptsize{of motion}}}\end{array}\left.\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{ll}q=|\!|{{\boldsymbol{q}}}|\!|\\[5.0pt]
\lambda={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{ll}\dot{q}=\{q,{{H}}\}+\tfrac{\uppartial q}{\uppartial{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{f}}}}}=0\\[5.0pt]
\dot{\lambda}=\{\lambda,{{H}}\}+\tfrac{\uppartial\lambda}{\uppartial{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{f}}}}}=0\end{array}\hskip 18.49988pt\Rightarrow\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{array}[]{ll}q=q_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\\[6.0pt]
\lambda=\lambda_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\end{array}

where
{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{f}}}}}=({{\boldsymbol{f}}},f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})
are arbitrary generalized perturbing forces (discussed soon).
That is, the constraint function,
\varphi=q-1
, and Lagrange multiplier,
\lambda={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
, turn out to be
integrals of motion of the transformed Hamiltonian system
even in the presence of arbitrary, perhaps nonconservative, forces
.
12
12

12

Note that
q
being an integral of motion is equivalent to
\varphi=q-1
being an integral of motion. This means that
q=q_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}={\mathrm{const.}}
is automatically satisfied. Enforcing the specific value
q=1
amounts to limiting consideration to initial conditions for which
q_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=1
.

Inverse Transformation & Interpreting the New Coordinates.

Eq.(
1.1
) is a (local) submersion,
{\Gamma}:{\mathbb{R}}^{8}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
, with
{\textrm{{\footnotesize{rnk}}}\hskip 1.42262pt}{\text{{{\small{{d}}}}}}{\Gamma}=6
and no global unique inverse. However, we may use the integrals of motion in Eq.(

■

‣
1.1
) to construct an inverse that is parameterized by some chosen, constant, values of
q=q_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}>0
and
\lambda=\lambda_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}
:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc}{\text{\scriptsize{restrict to}}}\\
{\text{\footnotesize{$q=q_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}$}}}\\
{\text{\footnotesize{$\lambda=\lambda_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}$}}}\end{array}\hskip 9.24994pt\Rightarrow\hskip 18.49988pt{\Gamma}^{\scriptscriptstyle{-\!1}}:({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})\mapsto({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}})\hskip 9.24994pt\left\{\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{llll}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\,=\,q_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}&,\hskip 9.24994pt{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\,=\,\tfrac{r}{q_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}({\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}+\lambda_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}\\[5.0pt]
u\,=\,1/r&,\hskip 9.24994ptp_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\,=\,-r^{2}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}\end{array}\right.

The above is
not
a true inverse of Eq.(
1.1
); it is only unique up to some chosen values
q_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}>0
and
\lambda_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}
(set by initial conditions).
We are free to limit consideration to any such values that we please as they place no restrictions on
({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})
.
We only consider the specific values

q=1
and
\lambda=0
, in which case the above
simplifies to:
13
13

13

q=1
ensures that
{{\boldsymbol{q}}}={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}
is the radial unit vector. The value
\lambda=0
is chosen because we see no advantage in considering any other value and because
\lambda=0
leads to
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
having a “nice” interpretation.

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc}{\text{\scriptsize{restrict to}}}\\
{\text{\footnotesize{$q=1$}}}\\
{\text{\footnotesize{$\lambda=0$}}}\end{array}\hskip 9.24994pt\Rightarrow\hskip 18.49988pt{\Gamma}^{\scriptscriptstyle{-\!1}}:({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})\mapsto({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}})\hskip 9.24994pt\left\{\hskip 9.24994pt\boxed{\begin{array}[]{llll}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\,=\,{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}&,\hskip 9.24994pt{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\,=\,-{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}\,=\,r({\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}\\[5.0pt]
u\,=\,1/r&,\hskip 9.24994ptp_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\,=\,-r^{2}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}\end{array}}\right.\begin{array}[]{llll}=\,r^{2}\dot{{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}}\\[6.0pt]
=\,-r^{2}\dot{r}\end{array}

One does not need to do anything to enforce the “constraints”
q=1
and
\lambda=0
other than to transform initial conditions
({{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}})\mapsto({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}})
using Eq.(
1.1
). This places no restrictions on
({{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}})
and it guarantees that
q_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=1
and
\lambda_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=0
. Since
q
and
\lambda
are integrals of motion, it also guarantees that
q=1
and
\lambda=0
hold for all time along any solution curve of the projective coordinate Hamiltonian system.

\,\scriptscriptstyle{\blacksquare}\,
Remark 1.1

(
Simplified Relation
)
.

We use “
\simeq
” to indicate relations which have been simplified using
q=1
and
\lambda={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=0
.
Note the latter is equivalent to
{{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=0
such that
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\simeq{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\simeq{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}
. That is,
{{\boldsymbol{q}}}
,
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
, and
{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}={{\boldsymbol{q}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
are mutually orthogonal and satisfy:

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{array}[]{lll}{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}={{\boldsymbol{q}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\\[4.0pt]
{\ell}^{2}=q^{2}p^{2}-({{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}})^{2}\\[4.0pt]
{{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}={{\boldsymbol{p}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}=0\end{array}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc}{\text{\footnotesize{using:}}}\\
q=1\\
\lambda={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=0\end{array}\Rightarrow\;\;\left\{\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{ll}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\simeq{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\simeq{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}\\[4.0pt]
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\simeq{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\\[4.0pt]
{\ell}^{2}\simeq p^{2}\end{array}\right.\;\;,\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{lll}{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\simeq-q^{2}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\simeq-{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\\[4.0pt]
{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\simeq p^{2}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\simeq{\ell}^{2}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\\[4.0pt]
{\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}\simeq{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}+{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}+{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}\end{array}\hskip 18.49988pt

In particular,
{{\boldsymbol{q}}}
and
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
directly define the inertial cartesian components of the local vertical local horizontal (LVLH) orthonormal basis associated with the particle’s instantaneous position:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lll}\begin{array}[]{cc}{\text{\scriptsize{orthonormal}}}\\
{\text{\scriptsize{LVLH basis}}}\end{array}\!=\,\{{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{t}}}}}_{r},{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{t}}}}}_{\tau},{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{t}}}}}_{\ell}\}=\{{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}\}\simeq\{{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}},{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}\}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt{\text{\scriptsize{with:}}}\;\;q^{2}\simeq 1\;,\;\;{\ell}^{2}\simeq p^{2}\end{array}

\,\scriptscriptstyle{\blacksquare}\,
Remark 1.2
.

We reiterate that the relations
q=1
and
\lambda={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=0
— which further imply those in Eq.(
1.1
)-Eq.(
1.1
) —
hold along
any
14
14

14

Specifically, any solution curve of the transformed Hamiltonian system, including arbitrary perturbations, for
{{H}}
in Eq.(
20
).

solution curve

({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}_{t},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}_{t})
with initial conditions
({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}})
satisfying
q_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=1
and
\lambda_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=0
. Equivalently, they hold along any solution curve with initial conditions
({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}})
satisfying Eq.(
1.1
) for some given
({{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}})
— this places no restrictions on
({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})
. Unless stated otherwise, Eq.(
1.1
) is always chosen as our default inverse transformation
({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})\mapsto({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}})
.

Transformed Dynamics & Generalized Forces.

When the projective coordinate transformation detailed above is combined with an appropriate evolution parameter transformation, the result fully linearizes Kepler-type dynamics (or Manev-type). First, we quickly note the time-parameterized dynamics and generalized forces for the projective coordinates.
The
t
-parameterized Hamiltonian dynamics in projective coordinates — which are still nonlinear — are obtained in he usual way using
{{H}}
from Eq.(
20
):

\displaystyle{{H}}=\tfrac{1}{2}u^{2}\big({\ell}^{2}+u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}\big)+V^{0}(u)

,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llll}\dot{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}=\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}\,=\,-u^{2}{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}&,\\[6.0pt]
\dot{u}=\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial p_{u}}\,=\,u^{4}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}&,\end{array}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{llll}\dot{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}=-\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}+{{\boldsymbol{f}}}&\!\!\!=\,-u^{2}{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}+{{\boldsymbol{f}}}\\[6.0pt]
\dot{p}_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}=-\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial u}+f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}&\!\!\!=\,-u({\ell}^{2}+2u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2})-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial u}+f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\end{array}

where
V^{0}
is the transformed central-force potential (using
r=1/u
), and
where
{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{f}}}}}=({{\boldsymbol{f}}},f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4}
are generalized perturbing forces.
They are obtained from the inertial cartesian components of the total perturbing force,
{{\boldsymbol{F}}}=-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{1}}{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}+{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, through the usual relations with the Jacobian of
{{\boldsymbol{r}}}({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}})
:

\displaystyle{{\boldsymbol{F}}}:=-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{1}}{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}+{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}}

,

\displaystyle\boxed{\begin{array}[]{lllll}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}:=\,-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{1}}{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}+{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}&\!\!=\,\tfrac{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsf{{T}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{uq}({\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\\[4.0pt]
f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}:=\,-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{1}}{\uppartial u}+\alpha_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}&\!\!=\,\tfrac{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}{\uppartial u}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\,=\,-\tfrac{1}{u^{2}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\end{array}}

,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llll}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}=0\\[3.0pt]
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}=\tfrac{1}{q}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}=\tfrac{1}{uq^{2}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\,\simeq\tfrac{1}{u}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\\[3.0pt]
{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}=\tfrac{1}{uq}{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\end{array}

where
-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{1}}{\uppartial{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}}=-\tfrac{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}{\uppartial{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsf{{T}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}\tfrac{\uppartial V^{1}}{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}
and
{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}}}=\tfrac{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}{\uppartial{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsf{{T}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{a}}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathrm{nc}}}}}
account for arbitrary conservative and nonconservative perturbations, respectively.
These terms also, individually, satisfy the above relations (e.g.,
{{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\rotatebox[origin]{15.0}{$\partial$}\mkern-2.0mu}_{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}V^{1}=0
and
{{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}=0
).
As mentioned at Eq.(
8
), any conservative perturbations would usually be accounted for simply by adding
V^{1}
itself to the Hamiltonian. That is, by adding
V^{1}({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},t)
to
{{H}}
, where
V^{1}({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},t)
is taken to mean the original
V^{1}({{\boldsymbol{r}}},t)
expressed in the new coordinates using
{{\boldsymbol{r}}}=\tfrac{1}{u}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}
. Yet, for the present purposes, it is more convenient to simply absorb any and all arbitrary perturbations into the cartesian components
{{\boldsymbol{F}}}
, which transforms to the above generalized forces
{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{f}}}}}=({{\boldsymbol{f}}},f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})
.

\,\scriptscriptstyle{\blacksquare}\,
Remark 1.3
.

If
{{\boldsymbol{F}}}
is purely radial, that is, if
{{\boldsymbol{F}}}=F_{r}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}=F_{r}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}
, then
{{\boldsymbol{f}}}=0
. As such, any central-forces of any kind — conservative or nonconservative — are absent from the ODEs for
({{\boldsymbol{q}}},{{\boldsymbol{p}}})
(rotational motion), appearing only in those for
(u,p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})
(radial motion).

Recovering Cartesian Coordinate Solutions.

Any dynamics presented in this work using the projective coordinates are for the transformation
({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}})\mapsto({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})
given in Eq.(
1.1
).
That same transformation can, of course, be used to recover
({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})
solutions from any
({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}})
solutions.
However, when converting numerical solutions, the transformation in Eq.(
1.1
) may be simplified considerably using the integrals of motion
q=1
and
\lambda={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=0
, leading to:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllllll}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{u}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\\[4.0pt]
{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}\,=\,-u({{\boldsymbol{q}}}\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\wedge\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}-u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\end{array}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt\xRightarrow[{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\,=\,0]{q\,=\,1}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt\boxed{\begin{array}[]{llll}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{t}\,\simeq\,\tfrac{1}{u_{t}}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{t}\\[4.0pt]
{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}_{t}\,\simeq\,u_{t}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{t}-w_{t}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{t}\end{array}}\hskip 18.49988ptw:=u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}

where
w:=u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}
is introduced for later convenience. The above still holds with
t
exchanged for any other evolution parameter of choice.
So long as
({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}_{t},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}_{t})
is a solution curve starting with
q_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=1
and
\lambda_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=0
, then
({{\boldsymbol{r}}}_{t},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}}_{t})
solutions recovered using the above simplified relations will be
numerically
equivalent to those recovered using the full projective transformation in Eq.(
1.1
).

Caution:
we stress that the simplified relations in Eq.(
1.1
) above are
not
the actual map for the projective transformation
({{\boldsymbol{q}}},u,{{\boldsymbol{p}}},p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})\mapsto({{\boldsymbol{r}}},{{\boldsymbol{{\nu}}}})
that should be used to transform the Hamiltonian and forces, obtain equations of motion, etc.
The actual transformation is given in Eq.(
1.1
).
Yet, when limiting consideration to solution curves satisfying
q=q_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=1
and
{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=0
(which we are always free to do), then, along such solution curves, the above is
numerically
equivalent to Eq.(
1.1
).

1.2  Central-Force Dynamics with a Transformation of the Evolution Parameter

We now use the projective coordinates described above in conjunction with a transformation the evolution parameter from the time,
t
, to two new evolution parameters,
s
and
\tau
, defined
by:
15
15

15

The evolution parameter
s
is the same as used by Burdet
[
1
,
2
]
,
\tau
(true anomaly) is equivalent to the one used by Vitins
[
3
]
, and Ferrándiz considered both
s
and
\tau

[
6
]
.

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}t\,=\,r^{2}{\mathrm{d}}s\,=\,u^{-2}{\mathrm{d}}s\hskip 18.49988pt,\hskip 18.49988pt{\mathrm{d}}t\,=\,\tfrac{r^{2}}{{\ell}}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\,=\,\tfrac{1}{{\ell}u^{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\hskip 18.49988pt,\hskip 18.49988pt{\mathrm{d}}\tau\,=\,{\ell}{\mathrm{d}}s

(69)

where
{\ell}^{2}=q^{2}p^{2}-({{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}})^{2}
and
where
\tau
is equivalent to the true anomaly (up to an additive constant). Using the notation
\dot{\square}:=\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\square}{\mathrm{d}t}
,
\acute{\square}:=\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\square}{\mathrm{d}s}
, and
\mathring{\square}:=\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\square}{\mathrm{d}\tau}
, we note that first-order ODEs transform simply by scaling:

\displaystyle\acute{\square}:=\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\square}{\mathrm{d}s}\,=\,\acute{t}\dot{\square}\hskip 18.49988pt,\hskip 18.49988pt\mathring{\square}:=\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\square}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\,=\,\mathring{t}\dot{\square}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}\acute{\square}

\displaystyle{\text{\footnotesize{with: }}}\hskip 9.24994pt\boxed{\acute{t}=r^{2}={1}/{u^{2}}\;\;,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathring{t}={r^{2}}/{{\ell}}={1}/{({\ell}u^{2})}}

(70)

That is, using the Hamiltonian dynamics in Eq.(
1.1
), the
s
-parameterized dynamics for
({\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}},{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}})=({{\boldsymbol{q}}},u,{{\boldsymbol{p}}},p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})
are given by:

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\acute{t}=\tfrac{1}{u^{2}}\;\;,\hskip 9.24994pt\left.\begin{array}[]{lll}\acute{{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}}=\acute{t}\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}}\\[5.0pt]
\acute{{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}}=\acute{t}(-\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}}+{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{f}}}}})\end{array}\right\}\hskip 9.24994pt\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllll}\acute{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}\,=\,-{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\\[5.0pt]
\acute{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}\,=\,-{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}+\acute{t}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}\end{array}\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{array}[]{lllll}\acute{u}\,=\,u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\\[5.0pt]
\acute{p}_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\,=\,-\tfrac{1}{u}\big({\ell}^{2}+2u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}\big)-\acute{t}\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial u}+\acute{t}f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\end{array}

with
{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{f}}}}}=({{\boldsymbol{f}}},f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})
as in Eq.(
1.1
).
The
\tau
-parameterized dynamics are obtained similarly, with
\mathring{t}
replacing
\acute{t}
:

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\mathring{t}=\tfrac{1}{{\ell}u^{2}}\;\;,\hskip 9.24994pt\left.\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathring{{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}}=\mathring{t}\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}}\\[5.0pt]
\mathring{{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}}=\mathring{t}(-\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}}+{\bar{{{\boldsymbol{f}}}}})\end{array}\right\}\hskip 9.24994pt\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllll}\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}\,=\,-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\\[5.0pt]
\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}\,=\,-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}+\mathring{t}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}\end{array}\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{array}[]{lllll}\mathring{u}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\\[5.0pt]
\mathring{p}_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\,=\,-\tfrac{1}{{\ell}u}\big({\ell}^{2}+2u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2}\big)-\mathring{t}\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial u}+\mathring{t}f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\end{array}

We note that, unlike the the
t
-parameterized ODEs in Eq.(
1.1
), the above
s
- or
\tau
-parameterized
ODEs in Eq.(
1.2
) or Eq.(
1.2
) are
not
true Hamiltonian systems (with nonconservative perturbations). They are
conformally
-Hamiltonian systems (with nonconservative perturbations) for the conformal scaling factors
\acute{t}
and
\mathring{t}
, respectively. Yet, by using the extended phase space, one may recover a Hamiltonian structure (with nonconservative perturbations) for the
s
- or
\tau
-parameterized dynamics. While this not overly important for the purposes of this work, the details are given in Appendix
D
.

•

A quasi-momenta coordinate.

Though not necessary,
it is often convenient to exchange the conjugate momentum coordinate
p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}
for a quasi-momentum coordinate
w:=u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}
. That is,

\displaystyle w:=\,u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\,=\,\acute{u}\,=\,-\dot{r}\hskip 9.24994pt\leftrightarrow\hskip 9.24994ptp_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\,=\,w/u^{2}\,=\,r^{2}w\,=\,-\acute{r}

(83)

For instance, if we replace the conjugate pair
(u,p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})
with the the non-conjugate pair
(u,w)
, then the above ODEs in Eq.(
1.2
) and Eq.(
1.2
) are equivalent to the following, more linear, ODEs:

\displaystyle\boxed{\begin{array}[]{llllll}\acute{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}\,=\,-{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}&,\hskip 9.24994pt\acute{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}\,=\,-{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}+\acute{t}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}\\[4.0pt]
\acute{u}\,=\,w&,\hskip 9.24994pt\acute{w}=-{\ell}^{2}u-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial u}+f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\end{array}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt{\text{\scriptsize{or,}}}\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{llllll}\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}\,=\,-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}&,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}\,=\,-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}+\mathring{t}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}\\[4.0pt]
\mathring{u}\,=\,w/{\ell}&,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathring{w}=-{\ell}u-\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial u}+\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\end{array}}

where the
({{\boldsymbol{q}}},{{\boldsymbol{p}}})
part is unchanged.
Interestingly, the coordinate
w=u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}
and the conformal scale factor
\acute{t}=1/u^{2}
cancel out in such a way that the above
s
-parameterized ODEs for
(u,w)
obey Hamilton’s canonical equations in their usual form (without the scale factor). That is, it can be verified that the above ODEs follow from:

\displaystyle{{H}}=\tfrac{1}{2}u^{2}({\ell}^{2}+u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}^{2})+V^{0}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{2}(u^{2}{\ell}^{2}+w^{2})+V^{0}

,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llllll}\acute{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}=\acute{t}\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}&,\hskip 9.24994pt\acute{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}=-\acute{t}\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}+\acute{t}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}\\[5.0pt]
\acute{u}=\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial w}&,\hskip 9.24994pt\acute{w}=-\tfrac{\uppartial{{H}}}{\uppartial u}+f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\end{array}

•

Simplifications.

None of the dynamics so far have been simplified with the integrals of motion
q=1
or
\lambda={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=0
, which imply the relations in Eq.(
1.1
) (e.g.,
{\ell}\simeq p
,
-{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\simeq{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
, and
{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\simeq{\ell}^{2}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}
).
As long as we limit consideration of initial conditions to those satisfying
q_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=1
and
\lambda_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=0
, then
we are free to simplify any of the above ODEs using the relations in Eq.(
1.1
). For example, the ODEs for
({{\boldsymbol{q}}},{{\boldsymbol{p}}})
simplify such that Eq.(
•
‣
1.2
) is equivalent to:

\displaystyle\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{array}[]{cc}q=1\\[2.0pt]
\lambda={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=0\end{array}$}}\Rightarrow\hskip 18.49988pt\left\{\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{lllll}\acute{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}\simeq{{\boldsymbol{p}}}&,\hskip 9.24994pt\acute{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}\simeq-{\ell}^{2}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}+\acute{t}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}\\[4.0pt]
\acute{u}=w&,\hskip 9.24994pt\acute{w}=-{\ell}^{2}u-\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial u}+f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\end{array}\right.

or,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllll}\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}\simeq\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\simeq{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}&,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}\simeq-{\ell}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}+\mathring{t}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}\\[4.0pt]
\mathring{u}\,=\,w/{\ell}&,\hskip 9.24994pt\mathring{w}=-{\ell}u-\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial u}+\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\end{array}

with
{\ell}^{2}\simeq p^{2}
.
We note that simplifying the dynamics such as above is
not
required in order to obtain linear dynamics in the unperturbed case that
{{\boldsymbol{F}}}=0=({{\boldsymbol{f}}},f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})
. When the perturbation terms are discarded, the
un
simplified ODEs in Eq.(
•
‣
1.2
) are already fully linear for certain forms of the central-force potential
V^{0}
(namely, for Kepler or Manev potentials).

Observe that the angular momentum plays a central role in the projective coordinate dynamics (in any of the above iterations). It is therefore useful to note that the ODEs governing the evolution of the various angular momentum functions from Eq.(
21
) are given in terms of the projective coordinates
as follows:
16
16

16

Note the relation
\dot{{\ell}}\simeq\dot{p}\simeq\tfrac{1}{u}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}
in Eq.(
1.2
) makes use of
q=1
and
{{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=0
(thus,
{\ell}\simeq p
).

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llll}\dot{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}={{\boldsymbol{q}}}\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\wedge\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{u}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\wedge\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}&\!(={{\boldsymbol{r}}}\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\wedge\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{F}}})\\[4.0pt]
\dot{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}\,=\,{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{u}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}&\!(={{\boldsymbol{r}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{F}}})\end{array}

,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllllll}\dot{{\ell}}&\!\!\!=\,\tfrac{q^{2}}{{\ell}}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}\,=\,\tfrac{1}{u}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\\[4.0pt]
&\!\!\!\simeq\,\dot{p}\,=\,{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}\simeq\tfrac{1}{u}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\end{array}

which confirms that angular momentum is conserved for any pure central-force dynamics (in which case
{{\boldsymbol{f}}}=0
, as per remark
1.3
).
Reparameterizing the above ODEs by
s
or
\tau

simply amounts to scaling by
\acute{t}=1/u^{2}
or
\mathring{t}=1/({\ell}u^{2})
.

Second-Order Dynamics.

Even without making use of the integrals of motion
q=1
or
{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=0
, the first-order
s
- or
\tau
-parameterized dynamics in Eq.(
1.2
), Eq.(
1.2
), or Eq.(
•
‣
1.2
) are equivalent to the following second-order dynamics for
({{\boldsymbol{q}}},u)

[
11
,
15
]
:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rllll}\hbox to0.0pt{$\;\,\acute{\vphantom{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\!\acute{\vphantom{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}$\hss}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\,+\,{\ell}^{2}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}&\!\!\!=\,\tfrac{q^{2}}{u^{2}}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}\\[6.0pt]
\hbox to0.0pt{$\;\,\acute{\vphantom{u}}\!\acute{\vphantom{u}}$\hss}u+{\ell}^{2}u+\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial u}&\!\!\!=\,f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}\end{array}

or,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rlllll}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\,\mathring{\vphantom{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\mkern 4.5mu\mathring{\vphantom{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}$\hss}}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\,+\,{{\boldsymbol{q}}}&\!\!\!=\,\tfrac{q^{2}}{u^{2}{\ell}^{2}}({\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}-\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}\\[6.0pt]
{\hbox to0.0pt{$\,\mathring{\vphantom{u}}\mkern 4.5mu\mathring{\vphantom{u}}$\hss}}u+u+\tfrac{1}{{\ell}^{2}}\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial u}&\!\!\!=\,\tfrac{1}{{\ell}^{2}}(f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}-\tfrac{q^{2}}{{\ell}u^{2}}\mathring{u}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{f}}})\end{array}

Or, using using Eq.(
1.1
) to express the right-hand-side in terms of the inertial cartesian components,
{{\boldsymbol{F}}}

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rllll}\hbox to0.0pt{$\;\,\acute{\vphantom{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\!\acute{\vphantom{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}$\hss}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\,+\,{\ell}^{2}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}&\!\!\!=\,\tfrac{q}{u^{3}}({\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\\[6.0pt]
\hbox to0.0pt{$\;\,\acute{\vphantom{u}}\!\acute{\vphantom{u}}$\hss}u+{\ell}^{2}u+\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial u}&\!\!\!=\,-\tfrac{1}{u^{2}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\end{array}

or,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rlllll}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\,\mathring{\vphantom{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\mkern 4.5mu\mathring{\vphantom{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}$\hss}}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\,+\,{{\boldsymbol{q}}}&\!\!\!=\,\tfrac{1}{qu^{3}{\ell}^{2}}(q^{2}{\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}-{{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}-\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\\[6.0pt]
{\hbox to0.0pt{$\,\mathring{\vphantom{u}}\mkern 4.5mu\mathring{\vphantom{u}}$\hss}}u+u+\tfrac{1}{{\ell}^{2}}\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial u}&\!\!\!=\,-\tfrac{1}{qu^{3}{\ell}^{2}}(u{{\boldsymbol{q}}}+\mathring{u}\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\end{array}

Simplifications.
If we use
q=1
and
\lambda={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=0
(implying the relations in Eq.(
1.1
)), then the second-order dynamics in Eq.(
1.2
) and Eq.(
1.2
) simplify to:

\displaystyle\;\;\begin{array}[]{rllll}\hbox to0.0pt{$\;\,\acute{\vphantom{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\!\acute{\vphantom{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}$\hss}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}+{\ell}^{2}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}&\!\!\!\simeq\,\tfrac{1}{u^{2}}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}&\!\!\simeq\,\tfrac{1}{u^{3}}({\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}-{{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\\[6.0pt]
\hbox to0.0pt{$\;\,\acute{\vphantom{u}}\!\acute{\vphantom{u}}$\hss}u+{\ell}^{2}u+\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial u}&\!\!\!=\,f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}&\!\!\simeq\,-\tfrac{1}{u^{2}}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\end{array}

or,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rlllll}{\hbox to0.0pt{$\,\mathring{\vphantom{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\mkern 4.5mu\mathring{\vphantom{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}$\hss}}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}+{{\boldsymbol{q}}}&\!\!\!\simeq\,\tfrac{1}{u^{2}{\ell}^{2}}({\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}-\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}&\!\!\simeq\,\tfrac{1}{u^{3}{\ell}^{2}}({\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}{\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\\[6.0pt]
{\hbox to0.0pt{$\,\mathring{\vphantom{u}}\mkern 4.5mu\mathring{\vphantom{u}}$\hss}}u+u+\tfrac{1}{{\ell}^{2}}\tfrac{\uppartial V^{0}}{\uppartial u}&\!\!\!\simeq\,\tfrac{1}{u^{2}{\ell}^{2}}(u^{2}f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}-\mathring{u}\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{f}}})&\!\!\simeq\,-\tfrac{1}{u^{3}{\ell}^{2}}(u{{\boldsymbol{q}}}+\mathring{u}\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}\end{array}\;\;

with
{\ell}\simeq p\simeq|\!|\acute{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}|\!|
and

\dot{{\ell}}\simeq\dot{p}={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{f}}}\simeq\tfrac{1}{u}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{F}}}
.

We note the above second-order dynamics only to clearly illustrate the relation to a perturbed linear harmonic oscillator, and for comparison with previous works. This work is primary concerned with first-order dynamics.

1.3  Rotational Motion Solutions for Arbitrary Central-Force Dynamics

The coordinates
({{\boldsymbol{q}}},{{\boldsymbol{p}}})
fully describe the rotational motion of the particle’s LVLH basis and closed-form solutions are immediately available for any central-force dynamics; recall from remark
1.3
that central-forces to do not participate in the dynamics for
({{\boldsymbol{q}}},{{\boldsymbol{p}}})
.
In contrast, the radial motion dynamics — described by the coordinates
(u,p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})
or, alternatively,
(u,w:=u^{2}p_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}})
— depend on the the particular form of the central force potential,
V^{0}
, and the radial perturbation term
f_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}=-\tfrac{1}{u^{2}}F_{r}
. Closed-form solutions are only available in certain cases, including the unperturbed Kepler problem (addressed in section
1.4
). Below, we consider the
({{\boldsymbol{q}}},{{\boldsymbol{p}}})
solutions for arbitrary central-force dynamics.

Let us consider, for the moment, the case that the arbitrary perturbing force,
{{\boldsymbol{F}}}
, is now purely radial.
Recall from remark
1.3
that
this leads to
{{\boldsymbol{f}}}=0
such that angular momentum is preserved:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc}{\text{\scriptsize{central-force}}}\\
{\text{\scriptsize{dynamics}}}\end{array}\!:

\displaystyle{\text{\footnotesize{if }}}\;{{\boldsymbol{F}}}=F_{r}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{r}}}}}\hskip 18.49988pt\Rightarrow\hskip 18.49988pt{{\boldsymbol{f}}}=0\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994ptf_{\scalebox{0.7}{$u$}}=-\tfrac{1}{u^{2}}F_{r}\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994pt\text{${{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}={{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}$}\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994pt\tau={\ell}s

where
\tau={\ell}s
(which assumes
\tau_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=s_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=0
) follows from preservation of
{\ell}
and the relation
{\mathrm{d}}\tau={\ell}{\mathrm{d}}s
.
Thus, for any arbitrary central forces, the first-order ODEs for
({{\boldsymbol{q}}},{{\boldsymbol{p}}})
in Eq.(
1.2
), Eq.(
1.2
), or Eq.(
•
‣
1.2
) are linear equations with constant coefficients:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cc}{\text{\scriptsize{central-force}}}\\
{\text{\scriptsize{dynamics}}}\end{array}\!:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllll}\acute{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}=-{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\\[4.0pt]
\acute{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}=-{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\end{array}\hskip 18.49988pt{\text{\scriptsize{or,}}}\hskip 18.49988pt\begin{array}[]{lllll}\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}=-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\\[4.0pt]
\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}=-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\end{array}

,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llll}{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}={{\boldsymbol{q}}}\raisebox{0.58122pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\wedge\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}={{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\in\mathfrak{so}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbb{R}}}}\end{array}

The solutions to either of the above coincide and are given by the matrix exponential
{\mathrm{e}}^{-{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}s}={\mathrm{e}}^{-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}\tau}\in\mathrm{SO}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbb{R}}}}
, which gives a special orthogonal rotation by
\tau
about
{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}
(the orbit normal direction). Using the
Rodrigues rotation formula
17
17

17

For any
{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with
{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}\in\mathfrak{so}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbb{R}}}}
, and some parameter
{\varepsilon}
, then the Rodrigues formula gives
{\mathrm{e}}^{{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\varepsilon}}\in\mathrm{SO}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbb{R}}}}
as follows (with
\rho=|\!|{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}|\!|
):

\displaystyle{\mathrm{e}}^{{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\varepsilon}}={\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}+\tfrac{1}{\rho}(\sin{\rho{\varepsilon}}){{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}+\tfrac{1}{\rho^{2}}(1-\cos{\rho{\varepsilon}}){{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}\,=\,{\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}+(\sin{\rho{\varepsilon}}){\hat{{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}+(1-\cos{\rho{\varepsilon}}){\hat{{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}

,

\displaystyle({\mathrm{e}}^{{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\varepsilon}})^{\scriptscriptstyle{-\!1}}=\text{${\mathrm{e}}^{-{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\varepsilon}}=({\mathrm{e}}^{{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\varepsilon}})^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\textsf{{T}}}}}$}

Note that if
\sigma:=\rho{\varepsilon}
then
{\mathrm{e}}^{{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}\sigma}={\mathrm{e}}^{{{\boldsymbol{\rho}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\varepsilon}}
.

,
along with some angular momentum relations in Appendix
A
,
this leads to solutions:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rllllll}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{s}={{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{\tau}&\!\!\!=\,R_{\tau}({\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\;=\;{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\cos{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\sin{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}\\[4.0pt]
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{s}={{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{\tau}&\!\!\!=\,R_{\tau}({\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\;=\;{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\cos{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\sin{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}\end{array}

,

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llll}R_{\tau}({\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}):={\mathrm{e}}^{-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}\tau}\in\mathrm{SO}^{3}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbb{R}}}}\end{array}\hskip 18.49988pt

The above verify that
{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}={{\boldsymbol{q}}}\raisebox{0.43057pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
,
|\!|{{\boldsymbol{q}}}|\!|
,
{{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
, and
|\!|{{\boldsymbol{p}}}|\!|
are preserved (i.e., integrals of motion) for any central-force dynamics:

\displaystyle{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}_{\tau}={{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{\tau}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{\tau}={{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\raisebox{0.38751pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\times\,}$}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}={{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\hskip 18.49988pt,\hskip 18.49988pt|\!|{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{\tau}|\!|=|\!|{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}|\!|\hskip 18.49988pt,\hskip 18.49988pt{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{\tau}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{\tau}={{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\hskip 18.49988pt,\hskip 18.49988pt|\!|{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{\tau}|\!|=|\!|{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}|\!|

(124)

As per Eq.(

■

‣
1.1
), preservation of
|\!|{{\boldsymbol{q}}}|\!|
and
{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}

Simplifications.
If we use
q=1
and
\lambda={\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=0
(implying
{\ell}\simeq p
along with the other relations in Eq.(
1.1
)), then the central-force dynamics and solutions for
({{\boldsymbol{q}}},{{\boldsymbol{p}}})
simplify to:

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{array}[]{cc}q=1\\
{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=0\end{array}$}}\;\Rightarrow\hskip 9.24994pt\left\{\;\;\begin{array}[]{lllll}\acute{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}\simeq{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\\[4.0pt]
\acute{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}\simeq-{\ell}^{2}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\end{array}\right.\;\;{\text{\footnotesize{ or,}}}\hskip 9.24994pt\begin{array}[]{lllll}\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}\simeq\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\\[4.0pt]
\mathring{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}\simeq-{\ell}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\end{array}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llllll}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{\tau}\,\simeq\,{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}\cos{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}+\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}\sin{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}&\!\!\simeq\,{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}\cos{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}+{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}}_{{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}\sin{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}\\[5.0pt]
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{\tau}\,\simeq\,{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}\cos{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}-{\ell}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}\sin{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}&\!\!\simeq\,{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}\cos{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}-p_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}}\sin{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}\end{array}

where
\tau={\ell}s
with
{\ell}={\ell}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\simeq p_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}=p
.
The above solutions also follow immediately from simplifying the solutions in Eq.(
1.3
).
It is again verified from the above that
{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}_{\tau}={{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}
.
Yet, unlike the unsimplified solutions in Eq.(
1.3
), the above simplified solutions do not
directly

|\!|{{\boldsymbol{q}}}|\!|
,

, or

iff

and

(which is what the above already assumes).
That is, using the above simplified solutions for
({{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{\tau},{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{\tau})
:

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}_{\tau}={{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}

,

\displaystyle\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{array}[]{cc}q_{0}=1\\
{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}}_{0}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{0}=0\end{array}$}}\;\Rightarrow\hskip 18.49988pt|\!|{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{\tau}|\!|\simeq|\!|{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}|\!|\simeq 1\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994pt{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{\tau}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{\tau}\simeq{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\simeq 0\hskip 9.24994pt,\hskip 9.24994pt|\!|{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{\tau}|\!|\simeq|\!|{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}|\!|\simeq{\ell}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\hskip 9.24994pt

As a Linear Hamiltonian System.

The above developments may also be combined into a single linear ODE for
({{\boldsymbol{q}}},{{\boldsymbol{p}}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{6}
:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{llll}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\end{pmatrix}$}}=L{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\end{pmatrix}$}}\hskip 18.49988pt{\text{\footnotesize{or,}}}\hskip 18.49988pt\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\end{pmatrix}$}}=\hat{L}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\end{pmatrix}$}}\end{array}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{s}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{s}\end{pmatrix}$}}={\mathrm{e}}^{\text{\footnotesize{$Ls$}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\end{pmatrix}$}}\;\;=\;\;{\mathrm{e}}^{\text{\footnotesize{$\hat{L}\tau$}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\end{pmatrix}$}}=\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{\tau}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{\tau}\end{pmatrix}$}}

where the matrices are given in terms of
{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}={{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}
and
\tau={\ell}s
by:

\displaystyle L\,,\;\hat{L}:=\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}L=\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}&0\\
0&-{\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}\end{pmatrix}$}}\in\mathfrak{sp}^{6}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbb{R}}}}\cap\mathfrak{so}^{6}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbb{R}}}}\hskip 18.00003pt,\hskip 18.00003pt{\mathrm{e}}^{\text{\footnotesize{$Ls$}}}={\mathrm{e}}^{\text{\footnotesize{$\hat{L}\tau$}}}=\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}R_{\tau}({\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}})&0\\
0&R_{\tau}({\hat{{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}}})\end{pmatrix}$}}\in\mathrm{Sp}^{6}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbb{R}}}}\cap\mathrm{SO}^{6}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbb{R}}}}

(136)

Interestingly, one may also express the above as a different, but equivalent, matrix ODE.
Using
-{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}=(q^{2}{\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}-{{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}
and
-{{\boldsymbol{{\ell}}}}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\star}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=-(p^{2}{\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}-{{\boldsymbol{p}}}{\raisebox{0.6458pt}{$\scriptstyle{\,\otimes\,}$}}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}){\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}
, the above dynamics for
({{\boldsymbol{q}}},{{\boldsymbol{p}}})
are equivalently expressed as:

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\end{pmatrix}$}}=A{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\end{pmatrix}$}}\hskip 18.49988pt{\text{\footnotesize{or,}}}\hskip 18.49988pt\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\end{pmatrix}$}}=\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}A{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\end{pmatrix}$}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{s}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{s}\end{pmatrix}$}}={\mathrm{e}}^{As}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\end{pmatrix}$}}\;\;=\;\;{\mathrm{e}}^{\frac{1}{{\ell}}A\tau}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\end{pmatrix}$}}=\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}_{\tau}\\
{{\boldsymbol{p}}}_{\tau}\end{pmatrix}$}}

(137)

where
A=A_{{\scalebox{0.7}{$0$}}}\in\mathfrak{sp}^{6}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbb{R}}}}
is again a matrix of integrals of motion (for any central-force dynamics), given as follows:

\displaystyle A:=\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}-({{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}){\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}&q^{2}{\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}\\
-p^{2}{\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}&({{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}){\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}\end{pmatrix}$}}\in\mathfrak{sp}^{6}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbb{R}}}}\hskip 18.00003pt,\hskip 18.00003pt{\mathrm{e}}^{As}={\mathrm{e}}^{\frac{1}{{\ell}}A\tau}=\text{\footnotesize{$\begin{pmatrix}\big(\cos{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}-\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}({{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}})\sin{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}\big){\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}&\big(\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}q^{2}\sin{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}\big){\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}\\
-\big(\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}p^{2}\sin{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}\big){\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}&\big(\cos{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}+\tfrac{1}{{\ell}}({{\boldsymbol{q}}}{\mkern 1.5mu\cdot\mkern 1.5mu}{{\boldsymbol{p}}})\sin{{\mkern-2.0mu}}{\tau}\big){\text{1}}\hskip-0.56905pt_{3}\end{pmatrix}$}}\in\mathrm{Sp}^{6}_{\scriptscriptstyle{{\mathbb{R}}}}

(138)

One can verify that any of the above matrix ODEs and solutions agree with those in Eq.(
1.3
).
